```
def reconstruct_mach_barycentric_cdi(t, T_map, S_map, barycenters,
                                     mach0, mach1,
                                     interp0, interp1,
                                     inside_body, domain_lo, domain_hi):
    """
    û(t,x) = (1−t) M₀( (1−t)x + t S(x) )  +  t M₁( t x + (1−t) T(x) )

    T = forward map μ₀→μ₁ (from g), S = backward map μ₁→μ₀ (from f).
    Gradient-free, exact at the endpoints: û(0)=M₀, û(1)=M₁.

    Query points are clamped to the mesh bounding box, then any point landing
    inside the solid body (exact predicate) or outside the Delaunay hull falls
    back to the UNDISPLACED endpoint value at the cell itself — i.e. the identity
    map locally — rather than to the nearest fluid cell to the garbage point (that
    nearest cell can sit on the wrong side of the airfoil).
    """
    mach0 = np.asarray(mach0, dtype=float)
    mach1 = np.asarray(mach1, dtype=float)
    if t <= 0.0:
        return mach0.copy()
    if t >= 1.0:
        return mach1.copy()

    W  = (1.0 - t) * barycenters + t * S_map        # pre-image sampled in M₀
    Tg = t * barycenters + (1.0 - t) * T_map        # pre-image sampled in M₁

    W  = np.clip(W,  domain_lo, domain_hi)
    Tg = np.clip(Tg, domain_lo, domain_hi)

    M0 = np.asarray(interp0(W),  dtype=float)
    M1 = np.asarray(interp1(Tg), dtype=float)

    bad0 = inside_body(W)  | ~np.isfinite(M0)
    bad1 = inside_body(Tg) | ~np.isfinite(M1)
    M0[bad0] = mach0[bad0]                          # identity-map fallback
    M1[bad1] = mach1[bad1]

    return (1.0 - t) * M0 + t * M1

28:# γ-annealing (ε-scaling): DECREASING list of γ for the IPFP, each stage
29:# warm-started from the previous (f,g).  Reaches small γ that diverge cold.
30:# Last entry = target γ used downstream.  Empty [] → single gamma_sb above.
31:#   • reference_drift = "null"    → keep γ_min ≈ 0.005: the pure-heat bridge is
32:#     ill-conditioned near the OT limit and the IPFP will NOT converge much below
33:#     this on the eig marginal (res stalls, kernel collapses → garbage maps).
34:#   • reference_drift = "oblique" → the drift conditions the bridge, so you can
35:#     anneal down to [0.0003, 0.0002, 0.0001] (that is the point of the drift).
36:gamma_sb_schedule = [0.02, 0.01, 0.005]
37:
38:# Reference drift β(t,x) conditioning the bridge (referrence_drift.tex).
39:#   "null"      — β≡0, pure-heat bridge (default; identical to previous behaviour)
40:#   "oblique"   — analytic θ-β-M diamond drift (windowed rigid shock rotations)

8	def get_dt(W, mesh, CFL=0.5, gamma=1.4, M=1.0):
9		Primitives = getPrimitive(W, gamma=gamma, M=M)
10		rho = Primitives[..., 0]
11		u = Primitives[..., 1]
12		v = Primitives[..., 2]
13		P = Primitives[..., 3]
14		celerity = jnp.sqrt(jnp.abs(gamma * P / rho)) / M
15		un = u[..., None] * mesh.normals[..., 0] + v[..., None] * mesh.normals[..., 1]
16		lambda_max = celerity[..., None] + jnp.abs(un)
17		dt_unstr = mesh.area / jnp.sum(lambda_max * mesh.surface[mesh.face_connectivity], axis=-1)
18		return jnp.min(dt_unstr) * CFL
19	
20	def get_dt_viscous(mesh, CFL = 0.5, nu= 1e-5):
21		dx_i = mesh.area / jnp.sum(mesh.surface[mesh.face_connectivity], axis = -1)
22		dt = jnp.min(CFL * dx_i**2 / nu)
23		return dt
24	
25	def getConserved(Primitives, gamma = 1.4, M = 1):
26		rho = Primitives[...,0]
27		u = Primitives[...,1]
28		v = Primitives[...,2]
29		P = Primitives[...,3]
30		Mass  = rho
31		Mom_x = rho * u 
32		Mom_y = rho * v 
33		Energy = P/((gamma-1) * M**2) + 0.5*rho*(u**2 + v**2)
34		W = jnp.stack([Mass, Mom_x, Mom_y, Energy], axis = -1)
35		return W
36	
37	def getPrimitive(W, gamma = 1.4, M = 1):
38		rho = W[...,0]
39		Mom_x = W[...,1]
40		Mom_y = W[...,2]
41		Energy = W[...,3]
42		u = Mom_x / rho 
43		v = Mom_y / rho 
44		P = (Energy - 0.5*rho * (u**2 + v**2)) * M**2 * (gamma-1)
45		Primitives = jnp.stack([rho, u, v, P], axis = -1)
46		return Primitives
47	
48	def get_specific_entropy(W, gamma = 1.4):
49	    rho = W[...,0]
50	    u = W[...,1] / rho

```
def build_cdi_interpolators(barycenters, mach0, mach1, barycenter_delaunay, mesh):
    """
    Build the CDI interpolators once (outside the frame loop).

    Returns (interp0, interp1, inside_body, domain_lo, domain_hi).  The nearest-
    neighbour interpolators are gone: a query point that lands inside the body or
    outside the mesh is no longer snapped to the nearest fluid cell (which can sit
    on the WRONG SIDE of the airfoil) — the reconstruction falls back to the
    undisplaced endpoint value instead.
    """
    interp0 = LinearNDInterpolator(barycenter_delaunay, mach0, fill_value=np.nan)
    interp1 = LinearNDInterpolator(barycenter_delaunay, mach1, fill_value=np.nan)
    inside_body = drift_mod.make_inside_body(mesh)
    bary = np.asarray(barycenters, dtype=float)
    return interp0, interp1, inside_body, bary.min(axis=0), bary.max(axis=0)


def reconstruct_mach_barycentric_cdi(t, T_map, S_map, barycenters,
                                     mach0, mach1,
                                     interp0, interp1,
                                     inside_body, domain_lo, domain_hi):
    """
    û(t,x) = (1−t) M₀( (1−t)x + t S(x) )  +  t M₁( t x + (1−t) T(x) )

    T = forward map μ₀→μ₁ (from g), S = backward map μ₁→μ₀ (from f).
    Gradient-free, exact at the endpoints: û(0)=M₀, û(1)=M₁.

    Query points are clamped to the mesh bounding box, then any point landing
    inside the solid body (exact predicate) or outside the Delaunay hull falls
    back to the UNDISPLACED endpoint value at the cell itself — i.e. the identity
    map locally — rather than to the nearest fluid cell to the garbage point (that
    nearest cell can sit on the wrong side of the airfoil).
    """
    mach0 = np.asarray(mach0, dtype=float)
    mach1 = np.asarray(mach1, dtype=float)
    if t <= 0.0:
        return mach0.copy()
    if t >= 1.0:
        return mach1.copy()

    W  = (1.0 - t) * barycenters + t * S_map        # pre-image sampled in M₀
```

def export_snapshot(W, mesh, t, cfg, out_dirs, helper, inlet=None):
    # Exporte les résultats sous forme de figures ou array numpy selon la config

    exp, gamma = cfg["export"], cfg["gamma"]
    snapshot_name = format_snapshot_name(cfg, t)
    diagnostics = None
    figure_fields = normalize_figure_fields(exp.get("figures", False))

    if exp["results"]:

        W_L = np.repeat(np.asarray(W)[..., None, :], 3, axis=-2)
        W_R = np.asarray(W)[np.asarray(mesh.neighbors)]

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Usage:
  uv run python slurm/run_euler_grid.py --case diamond --mesh-path meshes/diamond/diamond_h0.05.npy \
    --mach 0.7:1.5:0.1 --aoa 0:10:2
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
import copy
import itertools
import sys
from pathlib import Path

REPO_ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]
EULER_DIR = REPO_ROOT / "Euler"
for path in (REPO_ROOT, EULER_DIR):
    path_str = str(path)
    if path_str not in sys.path:
        sys.path.insert(0, path_str)

from Euler import main as euler_main
from Euler.config import load_mesh, setup_dirs, print_config


def parse_range(spec: str):
    if ":" in spec:
        start, stop, step = (float(x) for x in spec.split(":"))
        n_steps = int(round((stop - start) / step))
        return [round(start + i * step, 10) for i in range(n_steps + 1)]
    if "," in spec:
        return [round(float(x), 10) for x in spec.split(",") if x.strip()]
    return [round(float(spec), 10)]


def build_case_cfg(base_cfg, case, mach, aoa):
    cfg = copy.deepcopy(base_cfg)
    cfg["case"] = case
    cfg["Mach"] = round(float(mach), 10)
    cfg["aoa"] = 0.0 if case == "bump" else round(float(aoa), 10)
    cfg["stationarity_threshold"] = float(cfg.get("stationarity_threshold", 1e-5))
    return cfg


def main():
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    parser.add_argument("--case", choices=("bump", "diamond"), required=True)
    parser.add_argument("--mesh-path", required=True)
    parser.add_argument("--mach", default="0.7:1.5:0.1")
    parser.add_argument("--aoa", default="0:0:1")
    parser.add_argument("--tf", type=float, default=None)
```

A key finding before I ask: **SB cannot currently produce a solver-ready state at all.** `fields.npz` stores only `mach` and `press` — 2 of the 4 degrees of freedom. You can't recover ρ or the velocity direction from (M, p), so there's real work to do on the SB side first. The good news is the transport maps are field-agnostic and pressure is already pushed through them at [utils.py:2317-2341](SB/utils.py#L2317-L2341), so it's a small generalization.

    # is (physical rate)*dt and dt comes from the CFL — a finer mesh reports a
    # smaller residual for the same physical state and stops EARLIER in physical
    # time.  For a study comparing snapshots across meshes that is fatal.
    # --aoa is a diamond-only flag; it is meaningless for the bump
    AOA_ARGS=()
    [[ "$CASE" != "bump" ]] && AOA_ARGS=(--aoa "${R_AOA}")
    if uv run python Euler/main.py \
            --case "$CASE" --mach "${R_MACH}" ${AOA_ARGS[@]+"${AOA_ARGS[@]}"} \
            --mesh-path "${MESH}" \
            --flux HLLC --time-scheme SRK2 --reconstruction MUSCL \
            --stationarity-threshold 0; then
        N_OK=$(( N_OK + 1 ))
    else
        # Transonic/subsonic cases may genuinely fail to converge.  One bad Mach
        # must not take the other seven in the chunk down with it.
        echo "  [WARN] ${STEM} FAILED — continuing"
        N_FAIL=$(( N_FAIL + 1 ))

Resolution: this benchmark must pass a real threshold (start at `1e-6`,\nthe `config.toml` default). Do not change the sbatch convention — the existing corpus\ndepends on fixed-`tf` runs.",
  "new_string": "**3. The corpus passes `--stationarity-threshold 0`, deliberately.** The comment at\n[run_euler_grid_big.sbatch:237-242](run_euler_grid_big.sbatch#L237-L242) explains why:\nthe residual is normalised by *step count*, not elapsed time, so the criterion is\n`(physical rate)·dt` — a finer mesh reports a smaller residual for the same physical state\nand stops earlier in physical time, which is fatal for a cross-mesh study.\n\nThat caveat is about comparing **across meshes**. This benchmark compares four seeds on\n**one mesh at one pinned `dt`**, where the `dt` scaling is a shared constant and cancels —\nwhich is exactly why confound 1 must be resolved by pinning `dt` rather than by letting\neach seed pick its own. Resolution: pass a real threshold here (start at `1e-6`, the\n`config.toml` default) and leave the sbatch convention alone; the existing corpus depends\non fixed-`tf` runs."
}

def load_summary_rows(
    results_root: Path,
    case: str = "bump",
    mach: float | None = None,
    flux: str | None = None,
    reconstruction: str | None = None,
    time_scheme: str | None = None,
):
    results_root = Path(results_root)
    case_names = [case]
    rows = []
    expected_time_scheme = _normalize_time_scheme(time_scheme)

    for case_name in case_names:
        case_root = results_root / case_name

def setup_dirs(cfg, mesh):
    h_dir = format_h(mesh)
    case = str(cfg.get("case", ""))
    # LAYOUT PINNED to results/<case>/<h>/ with the AoA in the FILENAME.
    #
    # EulerSR writes data/raw/<case>/<h>/AOA<a>/ for AoA-carrying cases and
    # euler/results/<case>/<h>/ for the rest.  Adopting that here would:
    #   * orphan the ~700 diamond and bump bundles already on disk,
    #   * break the resume-skip in run_euler_grid_big.sbatch, which globs
    #     results/<case>/<h>/<stem>_*.npz -- so every resubmit re-solves
    #     everything while reporting COMPLETED,
    #   * break SB/make_config_for_mesh.py:find_bundle, which globs the same.
    # The AoA is already in the filename via format_condition_tag, so a
    # per-AoA directory would duplicate it.  Keep one convention.
    dirs = {
        "fig": repo_root / "figures" / case / h_dir,
        "res": repo_root / "results" / case / h_dir,
    }
    for directory in dirs.values():
        directory.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    if cfg.get("verbose", True):
        print(f"Output : {dirs['res']}")
    return dirs


def print_config(cfg, mesh, out_dirs):
    reconstruction = str(cfg["reconstruction"]).upper()
    time_scheme = str(cfg["time_scheme"]).upper()
--- format_snapshot_name ---
def format_condition_tag(cfg):
    mach = f"M{float(cfg.get('Mach')):.2f}"
    if str(cfg.get("case", "")).lower() in ("diamond", "naca0012", "naca2412", "rae2822", "onerad", "oa209"):
        aoa = float(cfg.get("aoa", 0.0))
        return f"AOA{aoa:.2f}_{mach}"
    return mach


def format_snapshot_name(cfg, t):
    condition_tag = format_condition_tag(cfg)
    flux = str(cfg["flux"]).upper()
    reconstruction = str(cfg["reconstruction"]).upper()
    time_scheme = str(cfg["time_scheme"]).upper()
    prefix = ""
    return f"{prefix}{condition_tag}_{flux}_{reconstruction}_{time_scheme}_t{t:.2f}"
```

## Approved Plan:
# Warm-starting the Euler solver from interpolated snapshots

## Context

The project's thesis is that SB interpolation lets you get CFD states without re-running
the PDE solver. The accuracy half of that claim is already measured — and it is negative:
SB does not beat the FFD baseline at any γ or any mesh. What has never been measured is
the **acceleration** half: if you seed the high-resolution Euler solve with an interpolated
field instead of uniform freestream, how many iterations does it save?

That is a separate and possibly stronger claim. A warm start does not have to be *accurate*
to be *useful* — it only has to be closer to the steady state than a uniform freestream, in
the basin of the same attractor. And it gives the three methods a second, physically
meaningful ranking axis that does not depend on the L2-vs-reference tables.

**Goal:** for each of the 9 target Mach numbers M2.05…M2.45 (diamond, h0.025), run the
solver four ways — cold (uniform freestream), Linear-seeded, FFD-seeded, BaryCDI-seeded —
and report iterations and wall time to the *same* steady state.

## The blocker

**SB cannot currently produce a solver-ready state.** `fields.npz` ([SB/utils.py:2498-2512](SB/utils.py#L2498-L2512))
stores `mach` and `press` only — 2 of 4 degrees of freedom. From (M, p) you cannot recover
ρ, and the velocity *direction* is gone entirely. The in-code comment at
[SB/utils.py:2312-2316](SB/utils.py#L2312-L2316) states the design decision plainly:
"SB reconstructs fields, never the conservative state W."

This is plumbing, not mathematics. The transport maps `T`/`S` are `(N,2)` absolute target
positions on cell barycenters — purely geometric, no Mach dependence — and the CDI fo
… [tronqué, 16180 caractères]

  "new_string": "    # An --out-tag run writes one level DOWN, into warmstart/<tag>/. }

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/anass/GenerativePDE/Euler/main.py",
  "old_string": "def run(W, mesh, inlet, cfg, out_dirs, export_mesh=None, parent=None):\n    # export_mesh/parent: solve on a nested solver mesh (`mesh`) but write the\n    # snapshot on its parent `export_mesh` -- see config.load_solver_mesh.\n    # Résolution numérique d'euler compressible\n    verbose = bool(cfg.get(\"verbose\", True))\n    W_initial = W",
  "new_string": "def run(W, mesh, inlet, cfg, out_dirs, export_mesh=None, parent=None,\n        W_freestream=None):\n    # export_mesh/parent: solve on a nested solver mesh (`mesh`) but write the\n    # snapshot on its parent `export_mesh` -- see config.load_solver_mesh.\n    # W_freestream: the uniform state initialize() would have produced.  Only set\n    # when W is a warm start; it keeps the entropy-creation diagnostic and the\n    # timestep referenced to freestream rather than to the seed.\n    # Résolution numérique d'euler compressible\n    verbose = bool(cfg.get(\"verbose\", True))\n    # delta_S must stay \"entropy created relative to freestream\", or a warm-started\n    # run's deltaS silently means something else and stops being comparable to the\n    # ~700 bundles already on disk.\n    W_initial = W if W_freestream is None else W_freestream"
}
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/anass/GenerativePDE/Euler/main.py",
  "old_string": "    dt = helper.get_dt(W, mesh, CFL=cfg[\"CFL\"], gamma=cfg[\"gamma\"], M=1.0)\n    N = int(cfg[\"tf\"] / dt) + 1",
  "new_string": "    # get_dt takes a GLOBAL min over cells, so a non-uniform warm start yields a\n    # different dt -- and therefore a different N and a different physical time per\n    # step -- than a cold run of the same case.  Comparing seeds then compares two\n    # clocks as well as two initial states.  --dt pins one value across the whole\n    # comparison.  Legitimate because for SRK2 the discrete steady state satisfies\n    # R(W*)=0 independently of dt: pinning dt changes the path, not the answer.\n    if cfg.get(\"dt\") is not None:\n        dt = jnp.asarray(float(cfg[\"dt\"]), dtype=W.dtype)\n        if verbose:\n            dt_nat = float(helper.get_dt(W, mesh, CFL=cfg[\"CFL\"], gamma=cfg[\"gamma\"], M=1.0))\n            print(f\"    dt imposé = {float(dt):.6e} (naturel pour cet état : {dt_nat:.6e})\")\n    else:\n        dt = helper.get_dt(W, mesh, CFL=cfg[\"CFL\"], gamma=cfg[\"gamma\"], M=1.0)\n    N = int(cfg[\"tf\"] / dt) + 1"
}
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/anass/GenerativePDE/Euler/main.py",
  "old_string": "    W, inlet = initialize(solver_mesh, CFG)\n    run(W, solver_mesh, inlet, CFG, out_dirs, export_mesh=mesh, parent=parent)",
  "new_string": "    W, inlet = initialize(solver_mesh, CFG)\n    # `inlet` is the farfield Dirichlet value and the sponge target: it stays the\n    # freestream state at the TARGET condition no matter what seeds the interior.\n    W_cold = W\n    if CFG.get(\"init_state\"):\n        W, init_meta = load_init_state(\n            CFG[\"init_state\"], mesh, CFG,\n            method=CFG.get(\"init_method\"), t=CFG.get(\"init_t\"),\n            dtype=W.dtype, parent=parent,\n            allow_repair=bool(CFG.get(\"init_allow_repair\", False)))\n        W = jnp.asarray(W)\n        CFG[\"init_meta\"] = init_meta\n        print(f\"État initial : {init_meta['init_kind']} {CFG['init_state']}\"\n              + (f\" [{init_meta.get('init_method')} @ t={init_meta.get('init_t')}]\"\n                 if init_meta.get(\"init_method\") else \"\")\n              + f\"  (rho_min={init_meta['init_rho_min']:.4f}, \"\n                f\"p_min={init_meta['init_p_min']:.4f})\")\n    run(W, solver_mesh, inlet, CFG, out_dirs, export_mesh=mesh, parent=parent,\n        W_freestream=W_cold)"
}

###########################################################################################################

@partial(jax.jit, static_argnames=("reconstruction", "flux", "numerical_flux", "flag_NS"))
def residual(W, mesh, **kwargs):
	# Calcul du résidu pour les équations d'Euler compressible 2D
	W_L, W_R = build_reconstruction(W, mesh, **kwargs)

	flux_type = get_flux_type(kwargs)
	if flux_type == 'tadmor':
		Flux = getFlux_Tadmor(W_L, W_R, mesh.normals, mesh.surface[mesh.face_connectivity], **kwargs)
	else:
		# FLux de Tadmor pour entropy stable, sinon flux classique
		use_entropy = kwargs.get('entropy', False)
		Flux = jax.lax.cond(
			use_entropy,
			lambda _: getFlux_Tadmor(W_L, W_R, mesh.normals, mesh.surface[mesh.face_connectivity], **kwargs),
			lambda _: getFlux(W_L, W_R, mesh.normals, mesh.surface[mesh.face_connectivity], **kwargs),
			operand=None,
		)
	sponge = helper.get_sponge_source(
		W,
		mesh,
		kwargs.get('value', jnp.array([1.0, 1.0, 0.0, 1.0])),
		gamma=kwargs.get('gamma', 1.4),
		M=kwargs.get('M', 1.0),
		width=kwargs.get('sponge_width', None),
		strength=kwargs.get('sponge_strength', None),
	)
	return Flux / mesh.area[...,None] - sponge

@partial(jax.jit, static_argnames=("reconstruction", "flux", "numerical_flux", "flag_NS"))
def time_step_Euler(W, mesh, dt, **kwargs):
	# Euler explicite (ordre 1)
```

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T08:53:02.642Z

Let me summarize where things stand while the calibration finishes.

**Done and verified:**

**Phase 1 — SB now exports a solver-ready state.** [SB/utils.py](SB/utils.py) — the CDI reconstruction is generalized to vector fields, `_build_warmstart_states` / `_assemble_conserved` push all four primitives through the same transport maps, and `warmstart.npz` + `maps.npz` are persisted behind a new `save_warmstart` key. Two checks passed:
- the scalar path is **bit-identical** to the original (`max|Δ| = 0` at every t), so published Mach/pressure metrics are untouched and I didn't need to duplicate the function
- **zero positivity clamps** across 222090 cell-frames

**Phase 2 — Euler accepts a seed.** New [Euler/warmstart.py](Euler/warmstart.py) loader with hard guards (all verified to fire: wrong Mach, wrong mesh, wrong cell count), `--init-state/--init-method/--init-t/--dt/--out-tag/--save-convergence` flags, and output redirected to `results/<case>/<h>/warmstart/<tag>/` so the corpus can't be clobbered. Zero new lint errors.

**Two measurements changed the design:**

**1. The solver has a residual floor — there is no tight steady state to race toward.** I seeded the solver with its *own* converged M2.00 bundle and ran 10619 more steps. The residual changed by **0.95×** (i.e. not at all) and the state moved by 2.6e-4. The corpus bundles sit at `‖R‖/‖R₀‖ ≈ 1.6e-2` and stay there.

This means the endpoint unit test **cannot pass at 1e-6** — the exact answer doesn't satisfy that criterion. It also means the operator-residual criterion would be *worse*, not better: it plateaus and never converges. And the reachable `rel` floor scales as `1/√(check_every)`, so threshold and check interval are coupled — which is why the corpus pairs `check_every=10000` with `1e-6`.

**2. The `lax.scan` tail is 8.4%, not the <1% I estimated.** A skipped step costs 3.2 ms against 38.4 ms for a real one, capping any early-stopped wall-clock speedup at ~12× regardless of seed quality.

**Both are resolved by one change rather than by restructuring the solver:** record the whole convergence curve (`--save-convergence`) and run every variant to the *same fixed N* with threshold 0. Nothing stops early, so trip count, compile and wall time are identical across variants and the tail confound cannot enter; "iterations to reach tolerance X" is read off afterwards for any X. [slurm/run_warmstart_bench.py](slurm/run_warmstart_bench.py) is built on this and self-calibrates the tolerance from the cold runs' floor.

Two calibration runs (cold vs Linear-seeded at M2.25, shared `dt`) are running now — they'll give the first real speedup number. Note this machine has no CUDA jaxlib, so each run is ~7 min on CPU; the full 36-run benchmark belongs on CALI3.

```
def _mach_output_dir(base_output_dir: str, cmach: dict, case_cfg: dict) -> str:
    case = cmach.get("case", "diamond")          # diamond | bump
    mode = cmach.get("density_mode", "mask")
    if mode in ("screened", "tv"):
        sub = os.path.join("denoising", mode)
    elif mode == "iso":
        sub = os.path.join("iso", "contours")
    elif mode == "iso_hessian":
        hmode = cmach.get("hessian_mode", "det")  # det | eig
        sub = os.path.join("iso", "hessian", hmode)
    elif mode == "field":
        fsrc = cmach.get("field_source", "grad_mach")
        sub = os.path.join("field", fsrc)
    elif mode in _MACH_MODE_FOLDER:
        sub = _MACH_MODE_FOLDER[mode]
    else:
        raise ValueError(f"unknown density_mode {mode!r}")

    # Keep each run's outputs separate: split by mesh resolution, then by drift
    # kind, then tag the smallest annealing γ used (so a new run never
    # overwrites a previous one).
    htag     = _mesh_h_tag(case_cfg.get("mesh", ""))
    root, axistag = _axis_root_and_tag(case_cfg)
    drift    = cmach.get("reference_drift", "null") or "null"
    # A seeded bootstrap is a different scheme from the heat-seeded one and must
    # not share its directory: tag it drift_SBsquared_exact+ffd etc.  Unseeded
    # runs keep their exact current paths.
    seed = str(cmach.get("drift_bootstrap_seed", "null") or "null").lower()
    if drift.startswith("SBsquared") and seed != "null":
        drift = f"{drift}+{seed}"
    schedule = cmach.get("gamma_sb_schedule") or [cmach.get("gamma_sb", 0.002)]
    gamma_min = min(schedule)
    gtag = f"gmin{gamma_min:g}"
    parts = [base_output_dir, root, case, sub, htag]
    if axistag:
        parts.append(axistag)
    parts += [f"drift_{drift}", gtag]
    return os.path.join(*parts)
```

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T08:56:29.789Z

Driver works — correct Mach mapping, shared `dt` per Mach, correct flags. Here are the commands.

**Step 1 — produce `warmstart.npz` (all three methods in one SB run).** Using the `SB_CONFIG` idiom so your `Config.toml` isn't mutated:

```bash
cd /home/anass/GenerativePDE

sed -e 's/^save_warmstart = false/save_warmstart = true/' \
    -e 's/^gamma_sb_schedule = .*/gamma_sb_schedule = [0.0003, 0.0002, 0.0001]/' \
    SB/Config.toml > /tmp/sb_warmstart.toml

SB_CONFIG=/tmp/sb_warmstart.toml JAX_ENABLE_X64=true uv run python SB/main.py
```

Writes to `outputs/Mach_interpolation/diamond/iso/hessian/eig/h0.025/drift_ffd/gmin0.0001/warmstart.npz` (~21 MB), containing `BaryCDI`, `Linear` and `FFD` — `ffd_control=true` means one run yields all three, no separate run per method.

**Step 2 — the benchmark:**

```bash
uv run python slurm/run_warmstart_bench.py \
  --case diamond \
  --mesh-path meshes/diamond/diamond_h0.025.npy \
  --warmstart outputs/Mach_interpolation/diamond/iso/hessian/eig/h0.025/drift_ffd/gmin0.0001/warmstart.npz \
  --t 0.1:0.9:0.1
```

36 solves (9 Mach × 4 variants) → `warmstart_bench.csv` plus a printed table of steps-to-tolerance, speedup vs cold, C_D, and repair counts.

**Three things to know before you launch it:**

- **Run it on CALI3, not here.** This machine has no CUDA jaxlib (JAX falls back to CPU at 38.4 ms/step), so 36 runs ≈ 4 hours locally versus ~15 min on GPU. Add `--dry-run` first to see the exact commands without solving.
- **Check the driver's calibrated tolerance in the output.** It self-calibrates to 3× the worst cold-run floor because the solver has a residual floor at `‖R‖/‖R₀‖ ≈ 1.6e-2` — a hardcoded `1e-6` is unreachable and every row would read `never`. Override with `--tol` once you've seen the curves.
- **Nothing writes into the corpus.** Output goes to `results/diamond/h0.025/warmstart/<tag>/`, one level below the non-recursive globs that `run_euler_grid_big.sbatch` and `find_bundle` use.

Useful variants: `--methods BaryCDI,FFD` to skip Linear, `--t 0.5` for a single-Mach smoke test (4 solves), `--repeat 3` for timing spread.

One caveat on the headline number: it is `steps_cold/steps_warm` at a fixed tolerance, converted to wall time via the measured per-step cost. That is deliberate — every variant runs the same fixed N so the `lax.scan` tail can't distort it — but it is a projection, not a stopwatch reading of an early-stopped run. The two calibration runs still going will give the first real data point on whether the gap is large enough to matter.

=== run_sb.sbatch header ===
#!/usr/bin/env bash
#SBATCH --job-name=sb-mach
#SBATCH --partition=gpu-l40
#SBATCH --nodes=1
#SBATCH --ntasks=1
#SBATCH --gres=gpu:1
#SBATCH --cpus-per-task=8
#SBATCH --mem=32G
#SBATCH --time=02:00:00
#SBATCH --output=logs/%x/%j.out
#SBATCH --error=logs/%x/%j.err

set -euo pipefail

mkdir -p "logs/${SLURM_JOB_NAME}"

# CALI3 environment: no `cuda`/`uv` modules exist here.  Real CUDA module is
# nvidia/cuda/12.4; uv is installed in ~/.local/bin (curl installer, no root).
module purge
# module load nvidia/cuda/12.4               # removed: shadows jax's cuDNN
export PATH="$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"
export LD_LIBRARY_PATH=""                     # ← add this line


export MPLBACKEND=Agg
export JAX_ENABLE_X64=true
export JAX_PLATFORM_NAME=gpu
export XLA_PYTHON_CLIENT_PREALLOCATE=false
export OMP_NUM_THREADS=$SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK
export MKL_NUM_THREADS=$SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK

JAX_CACHE_ROOT="${SLURM_TMPDIR:-/tmp}/jax-cache"
rm -rf "$JAX_CACHE_ROOT"
mkdir -p "$JAX_CACHE_ROOT"
export JAX_ENABLE_COMPILATION_CACHE=true
export JAX_COMPILATION_CACHE_DIR="$JAX_CACHE_ROOT"

echo "#############################"
echo "User:" $USER
echo "SLURM_JOBID:" $SLURM_JOBID
```

#!/usr/bin/env bash
#SBATCH --job-name=euler-big
#SBATCH --partition=gpu-l40,gpu-rtx6000,gpu-h100,gpu-a40
#SBATCH --nodes=1
#SBATCH --ntasks=1
#SBATCH --gres=gpu:1
#SBATCH --cpus-per-task=4
#SBATCH --mem=12G
#SBATCH --time=06:00:00
#SBATCH --array=0-60%12
#SBATCH --output=logs/euler-big_%A_%a.out
#SBATCH --error=logs/euler-big_%A_%a.err

# ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
# HR snapshot generation for the FULL Mach x AoA study.
#
#   sbatch run_euler_grid_big.sbatch                     # h0.025, everything
#   sbatch --array=0 run_euler_grid_big.sbatch           # smoke: one chunk
#   H_TAG=h0.0175 sbatch run_euler_grid_big.sbatch       # a finer mesh
#
# WHAT IT COVERS.  Every SB pair needs its two endpoints AND the 9 interior
# references at t=k/10, so a 0.10-wide Mach pair needs snapshots every 0.01:
#
#   Mach axis : M 0.80 -> 3.00 step 0.01, at each AoA in MACH_AXIS_AOAS   221/AoA
#   AoA  axis : AoA 0.0 -> 4.0 step 0.1 at M = FIXED_MACH                     41
#
# With the default AoA set {0, 2} that is 442 + 41 - 2 shared = 481 solves.
#
# CHUNKED, not one task per solve.  A single solve is ~15-30 s at h0.025 but
# `uv run` + JAX import + XLA compile costs ~40 s, so one-task-per-solve would
# spend more than half the allocation on startup.  Each task runs CHUNK pairs
# back to back instead; at CHUNK=8 the 481 solves become 61 tasks and the fixed
# cost is amortised ~8x.  Raise --array if you widen the grid: the script prints
# the required size and exits non-zero if the array is too small to cover it.
#
# THE TRANSONIC/SUBSONIC WARNING.  Below M=1 there are no shocks on the diamond
# at all, and between 1.0 and ~1.25 the leading-edge shock is DETACHED (a bow
# shock).  The Euler solver will still produce a field, but (a) it may not reach
# a steady state, and (b) the whole SB pipeline downstream keys on a Hessian
# SHOCK marginal.  Those runs are exploratory;

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T08:58:37.658Z

Syntax checks out and the path derivation matches your existing `gmin0.0001` dirs.

## Local (this machine)

No CUDA jaxlib here — JAX falls back to CPU at ~40 ms/step, so a full solve is ~7.5 min. **Use it for a smoke test, not the real run:**

```bash
cd /home/anass/GenerativePDE

# 1. seed file (all three methods in one SB run)
sed -e 's/^save_warmstart = false/save_warmstart = true/' \
    -e 's/^gamma_sb_schedule = .*/gamma_sb_schedule = [0.0003, 0.0002, 0.0001]/' \
    SB/Config.toml > /tmp/sb_warmstart.toml
SB_CONFIG=/tmp/sb_warmstart.toml JAX_ENABLE_X64=true uv run python SB/main.py

# 2. one Mach, 4 solves (~30 min on CPU)
uv run python slurm/run_warmstart_bench.py \
  --case diamond --mesh-path meshes/diamond/diamond_h0.025.npy \
  --warmstart outputs/Mach_interpolation/diamond/iso/hessian/eig/h0.025/drift_ffd/gmin0.0001/warmstart.npz \
  --t 0.5
```

Add `--dry-run` to print the solver commands without executing. The full `--t 0.1:0.9:0.1` sweep would be ~4.5 hours locally.

## Cluster (CALI3)

```bash
cd /scratch/aaboufadel/GenerativePDE      # home is full — run from scratch
sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch
```

That's the whole thing: it runs SB to produce `warmstart.npz`, then all 36 solves, then writes `warmstart_bench_diamond_h0.025.csv`. Roughly 40 min on an L40.

Variants via env vars:

```bash
TVALS=0.5 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch              # smoke: 4 solves
SKIP_SB=1 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch              # reuse existing warmstart.npz
METHODS=BaryCDI,FFD sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch    # drop the Linear baseline
REPEAT=3 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch               # timing spread
CASE=bump sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch
```

The sbatch follows your existing conventions — `module purge`, `LD_LIBRARY_PATH=""`, no system CUDA module, JAX compilation cache in `$SLURM_TMPDIR`. Two deliberate details: step 1 sets `JAX_ENABLE_X64=true` (SB's IPFP needs float64) and step 2 does **not** — the solver runs float32 and the loader casts the seed to match, because a float64 seed would double every buffer and trigger a separate compile, making the warm start look slow for a reason unrelated to seed quality.

**First real data point from the cold calibration run:** the convergence curve decays from 4.6e-4 and plateaus at ~7e-6 from step ~5500. Reaching tol=2e-5 takes **3700 steps**, tol=1e-5 takes **4575**. Anything below 5e-6 is unreachable. So there's a genuine ~3700-step budget for a warm start to eat into — the experiment has real dynamic range. The Linear-seeded comparison run is still going; I'll have the first speedup ratio when it lands.

}

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T08:59:14.135Z

End-to-end pipeline works, and the first real number is in.

**Cold vs Linear-seeded, M2.25, shared `dt`, both running the identical fixed N=11477:**

| tolerance | cold | Linear | speedup | steps saved |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1.0e-4 | 1250 | 25 | 50× | 1225 |
| 5.0e-5 | 2225 | 50 | 44× | 2175 |
| 2.0e-5 | 3700 | 675 | **5.5×** | 3025 |
| 1.0e-5 | 4575 | 1775 | 2.6× | 2800 |
| 8.0e-6 | 5150 | 2150 | 2.4× | 3000 |

The seed starts 8.7× closer than freestream (rel 5.2e-5 vs 4.6e-4).

**One caveat on how to read this, because it's easy to over-claim.** The speedup *ratio* collapses from 50× to 2.4× as the tolerance tightens — but the **steps saved plateaus at ~3000** across the whole usable range. That's the invariant. The warm start skips the initial transient; after that both runs decay at the same asymptotic rate into the same floor. So the honest statement is "**saves ~3000 steps, ~26% of the solve (~120 s of 463 s)**", not "5.5× faster" — 5.5× is an artifact of evaluating where cold happens to sit at 3700 steps. Pick the tolerance before looking at the ratio, or quote steps saved.

The fixed-N design held up: both runs reported N=11477 and wall times of 462.9 s vs 462.3 s (0.1% apart), so compile and trip count contributed nothing to the difference.

Worth noting this is **Linear** — the zero-transport baseline. Whether BaryCDI and FFD beat it is the actual experiment, and that needs the SB run (step 1 of the commands above). Given memory says SB loses to FFD on interpolation accuracy, the interesting question is whether the ranking is the same on this axis.

Nothing is committed, and the calibration runs wrote only to `results/diamond/h0.025/warmstart/`, clear of the corpus.

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T09:06:38.383Z

Classic SLURM failure: `--output=logs/%x/%j.out` requires `logs/warmstart-bench/` to exist *at submission* — the `mkdir -p` inside the script runs too late. You excluded `logs` from the rsync, so it isn't there.

Immediate fix:

```bash
mkdir -p logs/warmstart-bench
sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch
```

Confirm that was the cause with `sacct -j 1158036 --format=JobID,State,ExitCode,Reason`.

Let me make the sbatch not depend on a nested directory:

cd /scratch/aaboufadel/GenerativePDE
mkdir -p logs
sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch
tail -f logs/warmstart-bench_*.out
```

Log path is now flat (`logs/warmstart-bench_<jobid>.out`), so a single `mkdir -p logs` covers it. Your other sbatch scripts still use the nested `logs/%x/%j.out` form and will hit the same thing on a fresh scratch tree — `mkdir -p logs/sb-mach logs/euler-big` if you run those.

rsync -avz --exclude='warmstart/' ~/GenerativePDE/results/diamond/h0.025/ \

cd /scratch/aaboufadel/GenerativePDE && sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T10:33:34.136Z

rsync doesn't create intermediate parent directories. Use `--mkpath` (your rsync is 3.2.5, so it's supported):

```bash
rsync -avz --mkpath --exclude='warmstart/' ~/GenerativePDE/results/diamond/h0.025/ \
  aaboufadel@cali3.unilim.fr:/scratch/aaboufadel/GenerativePDE/results/diamond/h0.025/
```

If that flag errors on the sending side, create the path first instead:

```bash
ssh aaboufadel@cali3.unilim.fr 'mkdir -p /scratch/aaboufadel/GenerativePDE/results/diamond/h0.025'
```

then rerun the rsync without `--mkpath`.

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T10:45:56.228Z

Job's running correctly now. Analysis:

**SB step succeeded.** IPFP converged at all three γ stages (1712 iters, 44 s), and the warm-start block worked on the real maps: `clamped ρ/p = 0/0` for all three methods — the convexity argument holds, no repair fired. `warmstart.npz` 12.9 MB.

**On the SB-vs-FFD question, this run splits the difference by t:**

| metric | BaryCDI | Linear | FFD |
|---|---|---|---|
| W₂ @ t=0.1 | 1.60e-2 | 2.40e-2 | **5.05e-3** |
| W₂ @ t=0.9 | **9.55e-3** | 3.17e-2 | 2.27e-2 |
| L2_band (shock) | **1.06e-3** | 4.84e-3 | 1.11e-3 |
| Δβ (shock angle) | 0.91° | 2.20° | **0.25°** |
| mean\|ΔC_D\| | 0.29% | **0.17%** | 1.26% |

FFD wins the early frames, BaryCDI wins the late ones, crossover near t=0.5. On the transport-isolating metrics they're near-identical (1.06 vs 1.11e-3) and both ~4.5× better than Linear. Linear winning C_D is expected — the wall integral is where true transport is close to the identity, as your CLAUDE.md notes.

**⚠️ Do not read the wall times as a speedup.** Cold 12.36 s, then BaryCDI/Linear/FFD all *exactly* 9.59 s. That identical triple is the tell: the difference is the `lax.scan` XLA compile, which happens inside the timed region and is served from `JAX_COMPILATION_CACHE_DIR` for runs 2–4. Cold runs first at every `t`, so it pays the compile every time — a **systematic bias favouring the warm starts**. The real answer comes from the convergence curves, which are unaffected.

That's a defect in my driver's `time_to_tol_s`, so let me fix it:


## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T10:46:03.200Z

**Outil : Edit**
```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/anass/GenerativePDE/slurm/run_warmstart_bench.py",
  "old_string": "    for r in rows:\n        r[\"tol\"] = tol\n        r[\"steps_to_tol\"] = steps_to_tol(r.pop(\"_step\"), r.pop(\"_rel\"), tol)\n        r[\"time_to_tol_s\"] = (None if r[\"steps_to_tol\"] is None\n                              else r[\"steps_to_tol\"] * r[\"per_step_ms\"] / 1e3)",
  "new_string": "    # Per-step cost is shared across the variants of one t, NOT taken per run.\n    # Every variant executes the identical program (same mesh, same N, same dt,\n    # same static args), so any spread is measurement noise -- and it is biased,\n    # not random: the scan's XLA compile happens inside the timed region, and the\n    # first run at each t pays it while the rest are served from\n    # JAX_COMPILATION_CACHE_DIR.  Cold runs first, so charging each run its own\n    # wall time would systematically inflate the cold baseline and manufacture a\n    # speedup out of a cache hit.  The minimum is the compile-free estimate.\n    for r in rows:\n        r[\"tol\"] = tol\n        r[\"steps_to_tol\"] = steps_to_tol(r.pop(\"_step\"), r.pop(\"_rel\"), tol)\n    by_t = {}\n    for r in rows:\n        by_t.setdefault(r[\"t\"], []).append(r[\"per_step_ms\"])\n    for r in rows:\n        r[\"per_step_ms_raw\"] = r[\"per_step_ms\"]\n        r[\"per_step_ms\"] = min(by_t[r[\"t\"]])\n        r[\"time_to_tol_s\"] = (None if r[\"steps_to_tol\"] is None\n                              else r[\"steps_to_tol\"] * r[\"per_step_ms\"] / 1e3)"
}
```

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T10:46:24.864Z

Lint and parse clean.

**For the job currently running:** its `steps_to_tol` and `speedup` columns are computed from the convergence curves and are unaffected by this — those are the numbers to read. Just ignore `time_to_tol_s` in *this* CSV; it inherits the compile bias. Re-sync `slurm/run_warmstart_bench.py` before the next run and it's fixed (it now shares one compile-free per-step cost across the variants of each `t`, and keeps the raw value in `per_step_ms_raw` so the bias stays visible).

**Runtime:** 9.59 s for 11477 steps is **0.84 ms/step** on the L40, against 40 ms/step on CPU here — ~48×. So the full 36 solves land in ~6 minutes plus the 44 s SB run. It should already be done or close.

One thing worth watching in the output: every run so far reports `converged=False` with residual ~1.27e-5, which matches the residual floor I measured locally (`rel` plateaus around 7e-6 at `check_every=25`). That's expected and is exactly why the driver calibrates its tolerance from the cold runs' floor rather than using a fixed threshold — but do sanity-check the printed `Calibrated tolerance` line in the log. If any row comes back `never`, the tolerance landed below the floor and you'll want `--tol` set explicitly around 2e-5.

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T10:54:21.567Z

Your instinct about the times is right, but the GPU isn't the reason. All 36 runs execute sequentially inside one allocation on one node (`--nodes=1`, now yaga-10), so cold and warm see identical hardware — the GPU model sets the absolute ms/step but cancels in the cold-vs-warm ratio.

The actual unfairness was the compile cache, which I patched above: cold runs first at every `t` and pays the `lax.scan` compile inside the timed region while runs 2–4 hit the cache. That's what made cold 12.36 s against three identical 9.59 s.

And the headline metric doesn't touch wall time at all. `steps_to_tol` comes from the convergence curve — it's a count of iterations, identical on an RTX 6000, an L40 or your CPU. `speedup = steps_cold/steps_warm` is hardware-independent by construction. That's why I built it that way rather than timing early-stopped runs. So a separate cold calibration run wouldn't sharpen it.

Where GPU model *does* matter: converting steps into seconds. That's `per_step_ms`, and it's only comparable **across jobs** if the hardware is pinned. Your partition line lists four GPU types, so a rerun could land on an H100 and the absolute seconds wouldn't match. If you want quotable wall-clock savings:

```bash
sed -i 's/^#SBATCH --partition=.*/#SBATCH --partition=gpu-l40/' run_warmstart_bench.sbatch
REPEAT=3 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch
```

`REPEAT=3` also gives you the run-to-run spread, which you need before quoting any timing.

On the 17 minutes — that's not the solves. A solve is ~10 s but each is a fresh `uv run python Euler/main.py`, so ~40 s of interpreter + JAX import + XLA warm-up per run dominates: 36 × ~50 s ≈ 30 min. Expect it to finish around then. If that annoys you later, the driver could call `euler_main.run` in-process the way `slurm/run_euler_grid.py` does and cut it to a few minutes — worth doing if you scale to the full Mach-band sweep, not for 36 runs.

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T10:56:46.408Z

The log confirms the compile diagnosis definitively. Cold minus warm, per `t`:

```
t=0.1  12.36 − 9.59  = 2.77
t=0.2  12.48 − 9.74  = 2.74
t=0.3  12.62 − 9.85  = 2.77
t=0.4  12.78 − 10.00 = 2.78
t=0.5  12.91 − 10.13 = 2.78
t=0.6  13.04 − 10.25 = 2.79
t=0.7  13.18 − 10.40 = 2.78
t=0.8  13.34 − 10.54 = 2.80
```

A constant 2.78 s ± 0.03 across eight different Mach numbers and eight different `N`. Physics doesn't produce that — a cached XLA compile does. So the wall-time gap in this run is **entirely** the compile, zero of it is warm-start benefit. Good that the driver now shares one per-step cost across variants.

The slow upward drift (9.59 → 10.54) is just `dt` shrinking as Mach rises, so `N` grows.

Two things that *are* real in this log:

- **All four variants land on the same residual floor** (~1e-5, and ~8e-6 at t=0.5 for all four). That's the "same steady state" gate passing — a warm start that had converged somewhere else would show a different floor.
- **Every seed is admissible**: `rho_min ≈ 0.77–0.79`, `p_min ≈ 0.72–0.74` across all 27, no clamps.

The actual speedup isn't visible in this log at all — it's in the convergence curves. You're at 8.5 of 9 `t` values, so it should finish shortly. Then:

```bash
# on the cluster, confirm it wrote the table
tail -30 logs/warmstart-bench_1158168.out
```

The last thing the job prints is the calibrated tolerance and the steps-to-tolerance table. Pull it with the rsync commands from earlier and I'll go through the numbers — in particular whether BaryCDI's advantage over Linear on the transport metrics (L2_band 1.06e-3 vs 4.84e-3) actually converts into fewer solver iterations, and whether it separates from FFD at all.

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T11:00:36.831Z

Read it. Here's what it says.

```
  t     M  |  cold  BaryCDI  Linear   FFD      speedup (BaryCDI/Linear/FFD)
 0.1  2.05 |  3300      50      25     50       66x   132x    66x
 0.2  2.10 |  3225      75      75     75       43x    43x    43x
 0.3  2.15 |  3175     100     175     75       32x    18x    42x
 0.4  2.20 |  3150     100     275     75       32x    12x    42x
 0.5  2.25 |  3075     125     300    100       25x    10x    31x
 0.6  2.30 |  3075     100     225     75       31x    14x    41x
 0.7  2.35 |  3050     100     100     75       31x    31x    41x
 0.8  2.40 |  3050      75      50     75       41x    61x    41x
 0.9  2.45 |  3000      50      25     50       60x   120x    60x
```

**The warm start works, decisively.** Cold needs ~3000–3300 iterations; every seed needs 25–300.

**The shape is the real result.** Linear is V-shaped — 25 → 300 → 25 — exact at the endpoints it was built from, worst in the middle. BaryCDI and FFD are nearly flat (50–125, 50–100). That flatness *is* transport doing its job: at t=0.5, Linear needs 300 steps, the two transport methods need 100–125. That's the cleanest evidence in this whole study that the shock displacement matters, and it agrees with the transport metrics from the SB log (L2_band 1.06e-3/1.11e-3 vs Linear's 4.84e-3).

**SB does not beat FFD here either.** FFD is ≤ BaryCDI at every single t — equal at four, one check better at five. Same verdict as the interpolation-accuracy tables, now on an independent axis.

**But don't quote that last point yet.** `check_every=25` and the warm values are 50–125, i.e. 2–5 checks. FFD vs BaryCDI differ by *exactly one check* wherever they differ. The measurement quantum is 25 steps and the quantity is 100 — you cannot resolve a 25% gap at that granularity. The Linear-vs-transport gap (300 vs 100) is 8 quanta and is safe; the FFD-vs-BaryCDI gap is not.

Two more things:

- **All four land on the same floor** — `rel_min` spans 6.08e-6 to 6.66e-6 across every variant. Same steady state. That's the validity gate passing, even without C_D.
- **The absolute saving is 2.6 s per solve**, not 40× of anything wall-clock. Cold reaches tolerance at 3075 of 11434 steps, so cold-to-tolerance is 2.72 s out of a 10.1 s full run. The big ratio sits on a small base. Also visible: `per_step_ms` is 1.129 for cold vs 0.886 for the rest — that's the compile bias my fix removes.

For the rerun, the thing to change is resolution, not tolerance:

```bash
CHECK_EVERY=5 SKIP_SB=1 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch
```

Let the driver re-calibrate `TOL` itself rather than pinning it — shrinking the block raises the achievable `rel` floor (less oscillation cancels over 5 steps than over 25), so a tolerance calibrated at 25 won't transfer. That plus the `--summary` fix gives you 5-step granularity and the C_D gate, which is what's needed to say anything about FFD vs BaryCDI.

`ffd`, not null — the sbatch defaults to `DRIFT=ffd` and the log confirms it: `reference_drift='ffd' |β|_max=0.4081`, `[4/6] Anderson IPFP [drifted (ffd)]`.

So the `BaryCDI` column you just read is the SB solution *conditioned on the FFD drift*. Running `oblique` is a worthwhile A/B — it changes BaryCDI while leaving Linear and the FFD control untouched, so it isolates the drift's contribution.

But there's a collision that would silently corrupt that comparison: the benchmark's run tags are `ws_t0.50_BaryCDI` with no drift in the name, so an oblique run would overwrite the ffd run's outputs. Let me fix it:

Validated. `mach0_inlet`/`mach1_inlet` are set, and M2.00→2.50 is well clear of the ~1.25 detachment Mach, so oblique has an attached-shock solution.

Re-sync, then launch both:

```bash
rsync -avz --exclude={'.git','__pycache__','.ruff_cache','.venv','outputs','logs','results'} \
  ~/GenerativePDE/ aaboufadel@cali3.unilim.fr:/scratch/aaboufadel/GenerativePDE/
```

```bash
cd /scratch/aaboufadel/GenerativePDE

# redo ffd at finer resolution + C_D gate (seeds already on disk)
CHECK_EVERY=5 SKIP_SB=1 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch

# oblique — needs its own SB run, different drift means a different output dir
DRIFT=oblique CHECK_EVERY=5 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch
```

They're independent jobs now and won't collide: separate SB dirs (`drift_ffd/` vs `drift_oblique/`), separate run tags (`ws_ffd_*` vs `ws_oblique_*`), separate CSVs (`..._ffd.csv`, `..._oblique.csv`).

Two things to watch when they land:

**The FFD and Linear columns should be identical across the two CSVs.** `ffd_control=true` runs the registration regardless of drift, and both the FFD interpolant and Linear are γ- and drift-independent. If they differ, something is non-deterministic and the BaryCDI comparison isn't trustworthy either. That's a free correctness check.

**Only BaryCDI should move.** The difference between the two `BaryCDI` columns is the entire effect of swapping a data-driven drift for the analytic θ-β-M one — which is exactly the question you're asking.

Worth noting the oblique job will take a bit longer than the ffd one: it runs a fresh IPFP anneal (~45 s) plus, because `ffd_control` is on, the FFD registration too.

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T11:29:10.832Z

Verified — and the answer is yes, but it sharpens the caveat.

**Same state, confirmed:**

```
||W_linear − W_cold|| / ||W_cold||    = 2.66e-04
C_D   cold 0.0145186   linear 0.0145114   corpus 0.0145186
```

C_D agrees to 5 significant figures. And 2.66e-4 is not "close enough" hand-waving — it's *exactly* the solver's own limit-cycle amplitude. Earlier I measured that running the converged corpus bundle 10619 further steps moves the state by 2.56e-4. So cold and warm differ by the same amount the solver differs from *itself*. They land on the same attractor.

**Now the part you're right to be suspicious about.** The speedup is real but it is strongly tolerance-dependent, because the tolerance sits just below where the seed starts. At M2.25, `rel` at the first check is 4.58e-4 cold and 5.24e-5 warm. The auto-calibrated tolerance was 2.99e-5. So cold has to cross a factor of 15; the warm seed only a factor of 1.75. Sweeping the tolerance on the local traces:

```
tol      cold  linear  speedup
1.0e-4   1250      25    50.0x
5.0e-5   2225      50    44.5x
3.0e-5   3075     275    11.2x   <- what the cluster used
2.0e-5   3700     675     5.5x
1.0e-5   4575    1775     2.6x
8.0e-6   5150    2150     2.4x   <- at the floor
```

The ratio collapses from 50× to 2.4× as you approach the residual floor. **The invariant is steps saved (~2800–3000 across the whole range), not the ratio.**

Incidentally this cross-validates well: my local CPU run at tol 3.0e-5 gave cold 3075 / Linear 275; the cluster GPU run at tol 2.99e-5 gave cold 3075 / Linear 300. Different hardware, same answer.

So the defensible claim is *"a warm start removes ~2800–3000 of the ~3000–5000 iterations the transient costs, and the transport-based seeds hold that advantage in the middle of the range where Linear loses it"* — not "40× faster". If you want a single headline ratio, quote it at a tolerance you can justify physically rather than at the auto-calibrated one, and state the tolerance alongside it.

One thing the reruns will add that I can't check here: whether this state-agreement holds for **BaryCDI and FFD**, not just Linear. The `--summary` fix puts C_D in the CSV for every run, so that gate becomes automatic.

Both reruns finished, and there are three things in that output.

**1. The determinism check passed exactly as predicted.** `cold`, `Linear` and `FFD` are byte-identical across the ffd and oblique CSVs (2885/2840, 15/5, 30). They should be — none of them depends on the reference drift. That confirms the runs are reproducible and the two jobs didn't collide.

**2. The C_D gate is now working and it passes.** t=0.8: cold 0.01334 vs Linear 0.01333. t=0.9: all four at 0.01299. Four-to-five digit agreement, `init_repair_count = 0`. Same steady state, now verified automatically rather than by my one-off local check.

**3. The speedup ratio inflated, which settles the question you asked earlier.** Going from `check_every=25` to `5`:

```
             tol-hits          speedup
t=0.9  cold  3000 → 2840
       Linear  25 → 5        120x → 568x
t=0.8  Linear  50 → 15        61x → 192x
```

Nothing physical changed. Finer checks raise the achievable `rel` floor, the auto-calibration raises the tolerance with it, and the warm seeds cross that looser bar sooner. The ratio is tracking the measurement setup, not the solver.

But look at steps *saved*: 3000−25 = 2975, and 2840−5 = 2835. 2870 at t=0.8 either way. **Stable at ~2800–2975 regardless of `check_every`.** That's the real quantity, and it's the number to quote.

Both CSVs, same tolerance (3.36e-5), `check_every=5`:

```
  t     M  |  cold  Linear   FFD | BaryCDI(ffd)  BaryCDI(oblique)
 0.1  2.05 |  3060       5    30 |          30              25
 0.2  2.10 |  3020      30    40 |          50              45
 0.3  2.15 |  3015      65    50 |          65              60

 0.6  2.30 |  2885      90    55 |          80              75
 0.7  2.35 |  2875      50    55 |          70              65
 0.8  2.40 |  2885      15    50 |          55              50
 0.9  2.45 |  2840       5    30 |          30              30
```

**Consistency check: clean.** `cold`, `Linear` and `FFD` are identical in both files at all 9 points. The oblique job genuinely ran a different bridge (BaryCDI moved) while everything drift-independent reproduced exactly.

**Oblique beats ffd as the SB drift — consistently, but at the resolution limit.** −5 steps at seven points, −10 at t=0.5, tied at t=0.9. Every single non-tied point favours oblique (8/8, so the sign is unlikely to be chance), but each difference is *one check* at `check_every=5`. The direction is trustworthy; the magnitude isn't. If you want that quantified properly it needs `CHECK_EVERY=1`.

**FFD still beats SB, and by a resolvable margin this time.** In the mid-range where the seed actually has to work (t=0.4–0.6): FFD 55/60/55 against BaryCDI-oblique 75/75/75 and BaryCDI-ffd 80/85/80. That's 4–5 quanta, ~25–30%. Same verdict as the interpolation-error tables, now on the solver-acceleration axis. So SB loses to FFD on both.

**Linear's V is textbook**: best of everything at the endpoints (5 steps — it's exact there), worst in the middle (155). The transport methods stay flat (50–85) across the whole range. That flatness is the entire value of transport, and it's the cleanest demonstration of it in this study.

**The deflationary bottom line, though.** Against a cold baseline of ~2900 steps:

```
t=0.5   FFD saves 2835   BaryCDI-obl 2820   BaryCDI-ffd 2810   Linear 2740
```

Every seed removes ~97–98% of the transient. The spread *between* methods is 15–95 steps out of ~2800 — under 3% of the saving. So if the goal is purely solver acceleration, the choice of interpolant barely matters; even the zero-transport Linear blend captures nearly all of it. The method ranking is real and consistent, but it's a 3% effect on top of an effect that all three already saturate.

That's worth stating plainly in the writeup: warm-starting is clearly worth it, and it does not discriminate between the interpolation methods nearly as well as the L2/W₂/transport metrics do.

Now I have the real picture, and it reshapes item 4. **SB beats both baselines in 16 of 1576 runs (1%)** — and 15 of those are the *same band*: `naca0012 M0.80–0.90`. The one other is a diamond tie (ratio 0.991).

That band is **subsonic**. There's no shock below M=1, which is the regime CLAUDE.md shades red as exploratory, and the absolute errors there are ~2.0e-2 against 5e-4 for the supersonic diamond — 40× worse. So "where SB worked" is where all three methods are doing badly and the shock-transport premise doesn't hold. Worth knowing before spending cluster time on it.

```json
{
  "command": "uv run python -c \"\nimport json,glob,numpy as np,collections\nstat=collections.Counter(); rows=[]\nfor f in glob.glob('outputs/*_interpolation/**/metrics.json',recursive=True):\n    try: m=json.load(open(f))\n    except Exception: continue\n    e=m.get('errors') or {}\n    if 'BaryCDI' not in e: stat['no BaryCDI']+=1; continue\n    axis='AoA' if '/AoA_interpolation/' in f else 'Mach'\n    case=f.split('_interpolation/')[1].split('/')[0]\n    has=tuple(sorted(set(e)&{'BaryCDI','FFD','Linear'}))\n    stat[(axis,case,has)]+=1\n    rows.append((axis,case,e,m.get('config',{}),f))\nprint('run inventory (axis, case, methods present):')\nfor k,v in sorted(stat.items(),key=str): print('  ',k,v)\n\" 2>&1 | tail -25",
  "description": "Inventory all SB runs by axis, case, methods"
}
```

**Outil : Bash**
```json
{
  "command": "uv run python -c \"\nimport json,glob,numpy as np,collections\nagg=collections.defaultdict(lambda: collections.defaultdict(list))\nfor f in glob.glob('outputs/*_interpolation/**/metrics.json',recursive=True):\n    try: m=json.load(open(f))\n    except Exception: continue\n    e=m.get('errors') or {}\n    if not {'BaryCDI','FFD','Linear'} <= set(e): continue\n    axis='AoA' if '/AoA_interpolation/' in f else 'Mach'\n    case=f.split('_interpolation/')[1].split('/')[0]\n    k=(axis,case)\n    for met in ('l2','linf','w2'):\n        if all(met in e[v] for v in ('BaryCDI','FFD','Linear')):\n            vals={v:np.mean(e[v][met]) for v in ('BaryCDI','FFD','Linear')}\n            agg[k][met].append(vals)\n    tr=m.get('transport') or {}\n    if all(v in tr and 'l2_band' in tr[v] for v in ('BaryCDI','FFD','Linear')):\n        agg[k]['l2_band'].append({v:np.mean(tr[v]['l2_band']) for v in ('BaryCDI','FFD','Linear')})\nprint(f\\\"{'axis':>5} {'case':>9} {'metric':>8} {'n':>4} | {'SB wins vs FFD':>14} {'SB wins vs Lin':>14} | {'median SB/FFD':>13} {'median SB/Lin':>13}\\\")\nprint('-'*100)\nfor k in sorted(agg):\n    for met in ('l2','linf','w2','l2_band'):\n        L=agg[k][met]\n        if not L: continue\n        rf=np.array([d['BaryCDI']/d['FFD'] for d in L]); rl=np.array([d['BaryCDI']/d['Linear'] for d in L])\n        print(f'{k[0]:>5} {k[1]:>9} {met:>8} {len(L):>4} | {100*np.mean(rf<1):13.0f}% {100*np.mean(rl<1):13.0f}% | {np.median(rf):13.3f} {np.median(rl):13.3f}')\n\" 2>&1 | tail -30",
  "description": "Compare SB vs FFD and Linear across metrics, axes, cases"
}
```

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T11:39:11.599Z

Checked it properly now, and the data says the opposite. Across **all four drifts, 133 AoA runs**:

```
metric   drift              n | SB<FFD  SB<Lin | med SB/FFD  med SB/Lin
    l2   SBsquared_exact   12 |     0%      0% |     10.02       10.02
    l2   ffd               48 |     0%      0% |      4.71        4.53
    l2   null              37 |     0%      0% |      6.36        6.31
    l2   oblique           36 |     0%      0% |      5.33        4.80
```

SB never wins once on the AoA axis, and it's **4.5–10× worse** than both baselines. That matches your own note in memory: *"AoA-axis transport is amplitude-dominated, SB loses to Linear there."*

And there are **no bump AoA runs at all** — the bump inventory is 198 runs, all Mach. That's not an oversight: CLAUDE.md says the bump sits on a channel wall so AoA is meaningless, and `format_condition_tag` omits AoA from bump snapshot names entirely.

Full picture, so you can pick scope from data rather than recollection:

```
axis   case       n  | SB<FFD  SB<Lin | med SB/FFD  med SB/Lin
Mach   diamond  858  |     2%     53% |      2.03        0.92   <- SB's best showing
Mach   naca0012 387  |     5%     33% |      2.31        1.49
Mach   bump     198  |     0%     43% |      2.15        1.05
AoA    diamond  132  |     0%      0% |      6.36        6.27
AoA    naca0012   1  |     0%      0% |     14.27       39.53
```

SB's least-bad case is Mach+diamond — roughly tied with Linear, still 2× behind FFD. There is no case where it systematically beats FFD.

Given that, here's what I'd actually run:

1. **Tolerance sweep** — free, no solves. The script is written; it just needs the traces pulled.
2. **Diamond AoA** — worth doing *because* SB is 6× worse on interpolation there. If the warm-start speedup is still flat across methods, that proves speedup doesn't track interpolation accuracy, which is a real result. This is the most informative of the three.
3. **Bump Mach** — straightforward, already supported.
4. **naca0012 Mach** — the band where SB wins is `M0.80–0.90`, subsonic. Worth running, but the finding will be "SB wins where there's no shock and every method has 2e-2 error."

Bump AoA I'd drop as unphysical unless you disagree.

I'd stopped mid-edit on the driver's AoA-axis support when you interrupted — shall I finish that and set these up?

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T11:40:51.176Z

**Outil : Bash**
```json
{
  "command": "uv run python -c \"\nimport json,glob,numpy as np\nfs=sorted(glob.glob('outputs/Mach_interpolation/naca0012/iso/hessian/eig/h0.025/M1.80-1.90/*/*/metrics.json'))\nprint(f'{len(fs)} runs in naca0012 M1.80-1.90\\n')\nm0=json.load(open(fs[0]))\nprint('aero.methods[BaryCDI] keys:',list(m0['aero']['methods']['BaryCDI'].keys()))\nprint()\nprint(f\\\"{'drift':>22} {'gmin':>7} | {'meanl2':>26} | {'linf':>26} | {'mean|dC_D|':>26}\\\")\nprint(f\\\"{'':>22} {'':>7} | {'SB':>8}{'FFD':>9}{'Lin':>9} | {'SB':>8}{'FFD':>9}{'Lin':>9} | {'SB':>8}{'FFD':>9}{'Lin':>9}\\\")\nprint('-'*115)\nbest=[]\nfor f in fs:\n    m=json.load(open(f)); e=m['errors']; am=m['aero']['methods']; c=m['config']\n    V=('BaryCDI','FFD','Linear')\n    l2={v:np.mean(e[v]['l2']) for v in V}; li={v:np.mean(e[v]['linf']) for v in V}\n    cd={v:np.mean(np.abs(am[v]['dC_D'])) for v in V}\n    d=c.get('reference_drift','?'); g=c.get('gamma_final')\n    print(f'{str(d):>22} {g:>7} | {l2[\\\"BaryCDI\\\"]:8.2e}{l2[\\\"FFD\\\"]:9.2e}{l2[\\\"Linear\\\"]:9.2e} | {li[\\\"BaryCDI\\\"]:8.2e}{li[\\\"FFD\\\"]:9.2e}{li[\\\"Linear\\\"]:9.2e} | {cd[\\\"BaryCDI\\\"]:8.2e}{cd[\\\"FFD\\\"]:9.2e}{cd[\\\"Linear\\\"]:9.2e}')\n    best.append((d,g,l2,li,cd))\nprint()\nfor name,idx in (('L2',2),('Linf',3),('|dC_D|',4)):\n    nf=sum(1 for b in best if b[idx]['BaryCDI']<b[idx]['FFD'])\n    nl=sum(1 for b in best if b[idx]['BaryCDI']<b[idx]['Linear'])\n    print(f'  {name:>7}: SB beats FFD in {nf}/{len(best)},  SB beats Linear in {nl}/{len(best)}')\n\" 2>&1 | tail -30",
  "description": "Compare methods on naca M1.80-1.90"
}
```

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T11:41:13.386Z

You're right, and my mean-L2 ranking was the wrong lens. At **gmin=1e-4** in that band:

```
drift              | L∞:   SB      FFD     Linear | mean|dC_D|:  SB      FFD     Linear
null               |    3.13e-1  3.28e-1  3.17e-1 |           8.54e-4  1.23e-3  7.23e-4
SBsquared_exact    |    3.15e-1  3.28e-1  3.17e-1 |           9.35e-4  1.23e-3  7.23e-4
```

SB is **best of all three on L∞** there, and beats FFD on |ΔC_D| in 5/9 runs. So "SB wins" isn't confined to the subsonic band — I got that by ranking on mean L2, which SB loses 9/9 here. Two caveats worth carrying: it only holds at the fully annealed γ (at gmin 1e-3 and 1e-2 SB is 2–4× worse), and on C_D **Linear still beats both** (7.23e-4).

Crucially this band is **supersonic** — M1.8–1.9 is in the validated attached-shock regime, unlike M0.80–0.90. So it's a far better warm-start test case than the one I proposed.

```
"""
make_config_for_mesh.py — derive an SB Config for a different mesh resolution.

The Euler snapshot name embeds the final time (`…_HLLC_MUSCL_SRK2_t4.00.npz`),
and the solver's stationarity check stops different Mach runs at different times
(e.g. M2.35 → t2.74).  So the bundle paths for a refined mesh cannot be written
by hand — this script GLOBS the actual files produced by the Euler batch and
rewrites the `[run.mach.<case>]` block accordingly.

Everything else in the base config (drift, γ-schedule, density mode, …) is copied
verbatim, line for line, so the sweep sbatch can still `sed` its keys on top.

    uv run python SB/make_config_for_mesh.py --h-tag h0.0125 --out SB/Config_h0.0125.toml
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
import glob
import os
import re
import sys

# ── Interpolation axes ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────
# axis="mach": AoA fixed, Mach runs 2.00 → 2.50 (the original study).
# axis="aoa" : Mach fixed, AoA runs 0 → 4 deg.  Endpoints + 9 evenly spaced
#              references, matching the 11-frame reconstruction grid, so the
#              reference at t=k/10 is the physical snapshot at that parameter.
# Angle of attack is the axis on which LIFT is a real signal: at AoA=0 the
# reference C_L is mesh-asymmetry noise (~1e-4, and it flips sign between
# meshes), while over 0→4 deg C_L spans ~0 → 0.16.
MACH_ENDPOINTS = ("2.00", "2.50")
MACH_REFS = ("2.05", "2.10", "2.15", "2.20", "2.25", "2.30", "2.35", "2.40", "2.45")

AOA_ENDPOINTS = ("0.00", "4.00")
AOA_REFS = ("0.40", "0.80", "1.20", "1.60", "2.00", "2.40", "2.80", "3.20", "3.60")


def find_bundle(results_dir: str, mach: str, aoa: str = "0.00",
                case: str = "diamond") -> str:
    """The one .npz bundle for this (AoA, Mach), whatever final-time suffix it carries.

    The filename convention is case-dependent: ``Euler/config.py`` prefixes the AoA
    only for diamond, so bump snapshots are ``M2.00_...`` with no ``AOA`` 
… [tronqué, 99 caractères]

Reads the traces you already have. Gives speedup-vs-tolerance for all four methods plus a plot.

**2. naca0012 M1.80–1.90** — the band you identified. Needs a generated config, and `null` at gmin 1e-4 is where SB led on L∞:

```bash
uv run python SB/make_config_for_mesh.py --case naca0012 --h-tag h0.025 \
  --axis mach --p0 1.80 --p1 1.90 --out SB/Config_naca_M1.80-1.90.toml

BASE_CFG=SB/Config_naca_M1.80-1.90.toml CASE=naca0012 BAND=M1.80-1.90 \
  DRIFT=null CHECK_EVERY=5 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch
```

**3. Bump Mach:**

```bash
uv run python SB/make_config_for_mesh.py --case bump --h-tag h0.025 --axis mach \
  --p0 2.00 --p1 2.50 --out SB/Config_bump.toml
BASE_CFG=SB/Config_bump.toml CASE=bump CHECK_EVERY=5 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch
```

**4. Diamond AoA:**

```bash
uv run python SB/make_config_for_mesh.py --case diamond --h-tag h0.025 \
  --axis aoa --mach 2.00 --p0 0.00 --p1 4.00 --out SB/Config_diamond_aoa.toml

BASE_CFG=SB/Config_diamond_aoa.toml AXIS=aoa FIXED_MACH=2.00 \
  CHECK_EVERY=5 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch
```

**Check the bundles exist before launching** — that's what killed the first job, and none of these bundle sets are on your laptop so I can't verify them from here:

```bash
ls results/naca0012/h0.025/*M1.8*.npz results/naca0012/h0.025/*M1.9*.npz | wc -l   # want 11
ls results/bump/h0.025/*.npz | wc -l
ls results/diamond/h0.025/*.npz | grep -oE 'AOA[0-9.]+' | sort -u                  # want 0.00,0.40..4.00
```

`make_config_for_mesh.py` globs the bundles rather than constructing names, so if any are missing it'll tell you at config-generation time instead of inside the job.

On item 4 specifically: SB is 4.5–10× worse than both baselines on the AoA axis, so I'd expect its seed to be the worst of the three. If the warm-start speedup comes out flat across methods anyway, that's the most interesting result available here — it would show solver acceleration doesn't track interpolation accuracy, which reframes what the SB work is actually for.

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T11:45:57.527Z

Measured basis: a diamond h0.025 solve is ~10 s, but each run is a fresh `uv run python Euler/main.py`, so ~40 s of interpreter + JAX import + XLA compile dominates — ~50 s per run, 36 runs ≈ 30 min. The SB step was 44 s of IPFP plus registration, W₂ tables and ~40 plots, so ~5 min.

| job | SB | 36 solves | total |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tolerance sweep | – | – | **seconds**, no job |
| bump Mach (14178 cells, 0.70×) | ~5 min | ~25 min | **~30 min** |
| diamond AoA (20190 cells) | ~5 min | ~30 min | **~35 min** |
| naca0012 M1.80–1.90 | ~5 min | ~30 min? | **~35 min?** |

**Concurrently: ~35 min wall clock** plus queue. Sequentially ~1h40. The 4 h walltime in the sbatch is ample either way.

Two things I can't pin down from here: there's no `meshes/naca0012/` and no naca bundles on this machine, so the naca cell count is unknown. If that airfoil mesh is ~2× the diamond, its solves double — but since startup dominates, the job total only rises maybe 30%, not 2×.

**If you'd rather not pay 30 min of startup three times**, the driver currently spawns a subprocess per run; `slurm/run_euler_grid.py` instead imports `euler_main` and calls `run()` directly in-process. Switching to that amortises the JAX import and XLA cache across all 36 runs and would cut each job from ~30 min to ~8 min. Worth doing before three more jobs — say the word and I'll make it an option (keeping subprocess as the default, since it's the isolation the current results were produced under).

One caveat on the naca C_D column when it lands: memory says naca0012 reference C_D/C_L have a sawtooth versus Mach at plain `h0.025` because the detached bow shock spans only 1.3–5 cells, and the fix is the `le7` nose-refined tag. The existing SB runs used plain `h0.025`, so the warm-start numbers will be self-consistent, but the C_D validation gate against those references is softer than it is for the diamond.

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T11:47:11.396Z

Two things.

**The naca job will fail** — `make_config_for_mesh.py` errored (`mesh not found: meshes/naca0012/naca0012_h0.025.npy`) but the `sbatch` ran anyway since the commands weren't chained with `&&`. Job 1158249 has no config, so my guard should stop it cleanly:

```bash
grep FATAL logs/warmstart-bench_1158249.out
```

The naca mesh isn't on the cluster. Generate it, and check whether the bundles survived too — if the mesh is gone, they probably are as well, in which case naca needs an Euler grid run (11 solves for M1.80–1.90) before any of this:

```bash
uv run python Euler/generate_mesh.py --geom naca0012 -h 0.025 --out-dir meshes/naca0012
ls results/naca0012/h0.025/*.npz 2>/dev/null | wc -l      # need 11 covering M1.80–1.90
```

**The tolerance sweep is the real result, and it's decisive.**

At t=0.5, ranking is stable across the whole band: **FFD (160–290) > BaryCDI-oblique (210–480) > BaryCDI-ffd (235–510) > Linear (585–1245) > cold (3545–4265)**. FFD is ~1.5× ahead of SB, SB ~2.5× ahead of Linear. Oblique edges ffd for BaryCDI by ~10%, consistent with the earlier run.

But the stability table is the finding:

```
variant   speedup range      variation | saved range      variation
BaryCDI    6.4 –  66.5          10.5x  | 3240 – 4775         1.5x
FFD       12.8 –  67.8           5.3x  | 3300 – 4910         1.5x
Linear     2.9 – 677.0        230.7x   | 2915 – 4535         1.6x
```

Linear's "speedup" varies by **230×** purely with where you stop — 677× at the endpoints where it's nearly exact, 2.9× mid-range. Any single ratio you quote for it is meaningless. Meanwhile iterations saved sits at 2900–4900 for *all three methods*, varying only 1.5×.

So the two conclusions are:

1. **Never quote the speedup factor.** It's a property of the tolerance, not the seed.
2. **All three methods save essentially the same work** (~3000–4900 iterations) because they all remove essentially the entire transient. The method ranking is real and consistent, but it operates on the last few percent.

Also worth noting the floor moved as predicted: 1.205e-5 here at `check_every=5` versus 6.7e-6 at 25 locally — the ratio is √5 ≈ 2.2, matching the oscillation-cancellation model. That's why the sweep band is narrow (2.2e-5 → 1.27e-5) and why tolerance and check interval can't be chosen independently.

Bump and diamond-AoA don't depend on the naca fix — those two can go now.

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T11:51:32.795Z

The sbatch now self-provisions. Re-sync and launch all three:

```bash
rsync -avz --exclude={'.git','__pycache__','.ruff_cache','.venv','outputs','logs','results'} \
  ~/GenerativePDE/ aaboufadel@cali3.unilim.fr:/scratch/aaboufadel/GenerativePDE/
```

```bash
cd /scratch/aaboufadel/GenerativePDE
scancel 1158249 1158263

# naca0012, the M1.80-1.90 band where SB leads on L-inf
CASE=naca0012 BAND=M1.80-1.90 DRIFT=null CHECK_EVERY=5 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch

# bump, Mach axis
CASE=bump BAND=M2.00-2.50 CHECK_EVERY=5 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch

# diamond, AoA axis
AXIS=aoa BAND=A0.00-4.00 FIXED_MACH=2.00 CHECK_EVERY=5 sbatch run_warmstart_bench.sbatch
```

No `BASE_CFG` needed any more — each job now, in order: generates the mesh if absent, solves whichever of the 11 band snapshots are missing, generates the SB config, runs SB, then runs the 36 benchmark solves. Any step failing aborts the job with a `[FATAL]` line rather than letting the next stage run on missing inputs.

What I verified before handing this over: band parsing for all three forms (`M1.80-1.90`→1.80/1.90, `A0.00-4.00`→0.00/4.00 with refs at 0.40…3.60), and snapshot detection against your existing bundles — 11/11 found for diamond M2.00–2.50 including the odd `t2.74` one, and bump's no-AOA naming handled. `LC_ALL=C` is set, since a French locale would turn `%.2f` into `2,00` and break every glob.

**Time estimate changes for naca**: it has no bundles at all, so that job adds 11 Euler solves (~10 min) on top, and the mesh generation. Call it ~50 min versus ~30–35 for the other two. All three run concurrently.

`SKIP_EULER=1` if you ever want it to fail rather than silently spend 10 minutes solving.

One thing to watch on the naca job: it'll generate `naca0012_h0.025.npy` *without* `--nose-factor`, matching the plain `h0.025` your existing SB runs used. That's the right choice for comparability, but per your memory note the reference C_D/C_L on a rounded LE at plain h0.025 carry a sawtooth versus Mach — so treat naca's C_D validation column as softer evidence than the diamond's.

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T11:53:25.405Z

It isn't h100-only — it requests four partitions, same list as your `run_euler_grid_big.sbatch`:

```
#SBATCH --partition=gpu-l40,gpu-rtx6000,gpu-h100,gpu-a40
```

SLURM takes whichever frees first. Your last job proves it: 1158168 landed on `gpu-rtx60`/yaga-10, not an h100.

The one place h100-only could have crept in is the optional `sed` I suggested earlier to pin `--partition=gpu-l40` for cross-job wall-clock comparability. Don't apply it — and if you did run it on the cluster, the rsync just overwrote it with the four-partition list anyway.

Pinning isn't worth the queue wait here: the headline number is steps-to-tolerance, which is a count of iterations and identical on any GPU. Only `per_step_ms` is hardware-specific, and that's a secondary column.

## Claude  ·  2026-09-14T11:55:43.594Z

Step 0 is doing its job. The naca run auto-generated the mesh (**23572 cells**, 1.17× the diamond) and is now solving the 11 missing band snapshots — M1.84, M1.85, M1.86 at ~11.8 s each.

The two stray jobs died cleanly on the config guard rather than running on missing inputs, which is what it's there for.

Timeline for 1158273: ~9 min to finish the Euler snapshots, ~5 min SB, then 36 benchmark solves at ~12 s + startup ≈ 35 min. Call it 50 min total, comfortably inside the 4 h walltime. 1158274 and 1158275 (bump, diamond-AoA) will start as GPUs free.

I'd left the walltime edit unapplied — the running jobs keep their 4 h request, which is fine. Worth trimming to ~1h30 later only if queue waits become the bottleneck, since backfill schedules shorter requests into gaps.

Nothing to do but wait. When the three CSVs land, pull them and I'll compare — the interesting one is diamond-AoA, where SB's seed should be the *worst* of the three (4.5–10× behind on interpolation error). If it still saves ~the same iterations as FFD, that closes the loop: warm-start benefit saturates and doesn't track interpolation accuracy.
